# Bunzai page index

Generated catalogue of documentation collections, pages, and nested sections.
Semantic summaries are navigation aids; read the source page before relying on a detail.
Only independently APPROVED semantic sidecars are merged into this catalogue.

## (root)

### Vision

- id: `01-VISION`
- path: `docs/01-VISION.md`
- Founding product vision for Bunzai covering who it's for, the five pillars, what we are not, and the taste bar. It is the authority for product intent, not as-built implementation detail. Who it's for names four personas: the first-timer needing a five-minute fix, the multi-platform botmaker, the large-worldbook lore architect, and the archivist of uncarded RP logs. The five pillars require lossless multi-format conversion with escrowed platform fields, interview-driven creation, an honest offline Doctor with optional staged AI fixes, local-first privacy with no account or telemetry, and an agent that works on frontier and weak local models with user-chosen personas. What we are not rules out being a chat frontend, card host, community platform, or RoleCall-only funnel; every platform is first-class. The taste bar demands Table Read or Doctor output that beats skilled unaided work, with specific over generic, playable over poetic, and anti-slop as a core mechanic rather than marketing language.

- **H2 Who it's for** (`who-its-for`)
  Defines four concrete user personas the product must serve without forcing power-user complexity on beginners. The first-timer found a broken Discord card and must succeed in under five minutes without reading docs. The botmaker publishes community cards and currently juggles multiple platform formats, token budgets, and slop avoidance by hand. The lore architect maintains roughly three-hundred-entry worldbooks and needs trigger debugging, consistency checks, and real version history. The archivist holds years of RP logs with characters that were never formalized as cards. The framing is simple for simple folk and powerful for advanced users.
- **H2 The five pillars** (`the-five-pillars`)
  States five non-negotiable product pillars. Every format must import and export losslessly with semantic round-trip equivalence and platform-specific fields held in escrow rather than dropped, aiming to be the reference implementation other tools test against. Creation is interview-driven with adaptive depth, quick-pick chips, freeform input, visible assembly, and temperament modes. The Doctor runs free offline deterministic checks for token waste, format errors, contradictions, and slop banks, while AI treatment is optional, staged, and approved fix by fix with plain-language proof. Local first means readable disk files, encrypted local keys, no account, no server, no telemetry, and a single executable as the whole product. The agent must run well on frontier models and acceptably on small local models, with user-chosen working styles such as collaborator, terse, detail-hound, challenger, or hype.
- **H2 What we are not** (`what-we-are-not`)
  Explicit non-goals that bound the product. Bunzai is not a chat frontend; the Test Stage auditions content and does not replace SillyTavern or RoleCall as play hosts. It is not a card hosting site or community platform; files are the social layer and people share where they already gather. It is not a RoleCall funnel: lineage may be RC-branded, but every platform is a first-class export target, and favoritism toward RoleCall would lose SillyTavern users. Winning is defined as making the content, not hosting the show.
- **H2 The taste bar** (`the-taste-bar`)
  Sets the craft quality bar for generated or treated content. A card from the Table Read or treated by the Doctor should be visibly better than what a skilled human produces unaided in an hour. Preferences are specific over generic, playable over poetic, and every token must earn its place. Anti-slop is a core mechanic implemented through banks, detectors, and rewrite passes, not merely marketing language about quality.

### Architecture

- id: `02-ARCHITECTURE`
- path: `docs/02-ARCHITECTURE.md`
- This page defines Bunzai's implemented architecture and the boundaries contributors must preserve. Source boundaries assign the shared engine to src/core, entity schemas to src/entities, drop-in adapters to src/formats, durable local storage to src/studio, sealed-script analysis to src/sandbox, the primary loopback React application to src/ui, Kit's agent loop to src/kit, and the command surface to src/cli.ts. Canonical model and formats explains the hub-and-spoke superset, original source escrow, sparse platform profiles, same-format re-projection, cross-format loss reports, and folder-discovered adapters. Storage and mutation describes the folder database, contained paths, atomic writes, revisions, pure previews, and verified durable application. The three surfaces are the scriptable bunzai CLI, the local desktop Studio with separately enabled remote access, and source-checkout Kit, all sharing one engine while Kit remains absent from release binaries until its build and smoke gates exist. Effects and trust boundaries require fail-closed parsing, sealed imported scripts, deliberate network paths, and encrypted provider credentials that never enter content or model-visible records. Kit checks the initial provider request host but does not yet revalidate runtime redirects. Decisions and future systems points to ADR-001 onward and says specs remain planned rather than implemented until source, tests, and a user entry point prove otherwise.

- **H2 Source boundaries** (`source-boundaries`)
  Bunzai is a Bun and TypeScript repository rooted in src, with three user surfaces built on one shared engine. src/core owns canonical entities, detection, conversion, coverage, and semantic engines. src/entities owns per-kind schemas and pure capabilities. src/formats contains one drop-in adapter folder per external family. src/studio is the durable local folder database. src/sandbox owns sealed-script analysis and user-invoked benches. src/ui contains the loopback server and React Studio. src/kit contains the source-checkout agent loop, provider adapters, discovery, staged drafts, and transcript ledger. src/cli.ts is the command entry. Dependencies point inward: surface code may depend on engine code, while engine code must never depend on a surface.
- **H2 Canonical model and formats** (`canonical-model-and-formats`)
  Every supported file maps through the canonical model in src/core/canonical.ts; no format converts directly to another format. The canonical body is a superset of interoperable wire fields. Each imported entity also retains its parsed source snapshot and unmapped fields in original escrow, while sparse profiles preserve platform-specific overrides. Same-format export re-projects edits over that source twin so untouched details survive. Cross-format export carries only supported canonical meaning and reports escrowed, dropped, shadowed, or warned paths rather than silently claiming portability. Format adapters are folders under src/formats and expose detect, toCanonical, and fromCanonical. The loader discovers those folders, and the registry, CLI formats listing, Studio import surfaces, and support matrix all consume the same live adapter set.
- **H2 Storage and mutation** (`storage-and-mutation`)
  Studio storage is a folder database rather than an opaque application database. Each piece is saved as readable JSON beneath a per-kind and per-id path. The storage layer contains paths to the studio root, writes atomically, supports keep-both collision behavior, and compares expected revisions before saving so concurrent edits fail visibly. Semantic capability operations remain pure: they produce previews and validation evidence without touching disk. A separate apply path performs the authorized durable mutation, then re-reads the saved entity to verify the result and produce trustworthy post-write evidence. This separation lets CLI, Studio, and Kit share edit logic without granting preview code an accidental write path.
- **H2 The three surfaces** (`the-three-surfaces`)
  Bunzai exposes three faces over the same engine. The bunzai command is the scriptable CLI for conversion, inspection, validation, labeling, discovery, and Studio launch. Studio is the local desktop experience: a React client served by a Bun loopback server, with the remote-access sidecar remaining an explicit opt-in. Kit is the source-checkout conversational surface. It connects a configured model provider, progressively discovers content and documentation tools, accumulates staged changes with warnings and platform impact, and applies only through the same durable storage boundary. Kit is real source code but is not yet part of distributed release binaries; release claims must wait for a defined bundle, binary inclusion, and installed-artifact smoke test.
- **H2 Effects and trust boundaries** (`effects-and-trust-boundaries`)
  Untrusted files and requests are parsed once at their boundary and rejected when unreadable. Imported Lua, macros, and regex payloads remain inert data during import, conversion, storage, preview, and agent use. Execution exists only in the explicit Lua or regex test benches on the isolated sandbox origin, and no additional execution path is allowed. Network effects are deliberate and auditable: remote Studio access is opt-in, Kit checks the initial provider request URL against the configured host, and update checks target fixed GitHub release infrastructure. The current Kit fetch path does not revalidate runtime redirects, so its host gate is not an end-to-end redirect policy. Bunzai has no accounts, telemetry, proxying, or background upload. Provider credentials are encrypted at rest and are configuration, never content: they must not appear in canonical entities, prompts, tool observations, exports, transcripts, or the ledger.
- **H2 Decisions and future systems** (`decisions-and-future-systems`)
  Consequential architectural choices begin with ADR-001 in docs/decisions and continue through the repository's current decision records. Detailed contracts for implemented and future areas live under specs. A specification, roadmap entry, or design description does not by itself establish a shipped capability. Future systems remain explicitly planned until matching source code exists, tests prove the contract, and users have a real entry point. Reference documentation must describe that implemented state honestly rather than converting architectural intent into a release claim.

### Conventions

- id: `03-CONVENTIONS`
- path: `docs/03-CONVENTIONS.md`
- This page states Bunzai's binding current implementation rules. Code requires strict TypeScript boundary narrowing, exact per-kind runtime decoders for handwritten canonical interfaces, explicitly declared open escrow bags, UI-independent core logic, typed errors, consistent naming, and the scoped TS/TSX pictograph gate. defineExhaustiveShape<T>() rejects missing and extra keys, narrowed unions, coercion, any at any nesting depth, and never during typecheck. Tests use Bun across authored src and scripts, begin bug fixes at a failing public boundary, prove semantic same-format round trips, remain deterministic, and do not treat synchronization warnings as clean proof. CLI defines the unfinished automation target for convert, inspect, validate, label, formats, and version. Repository requires useful release-history subjects and behavioral evidence while forbidding gate bypasses. Documentation separates built reference behavior from labeled plans and moves with source. Working method requires inspecting the owning system and Git state, fixing the real owner, reporting verification limits, and obtaining independent review without self-approval. The runtime pin and shared virtualization boundary are explicit repository conventions.

- **H2 Code** (`code`)
  TypeScript runs in strict mode, and untrusted values are narrowed at entry. Handwritten domain interfaces may remain rich internal contracts, but separate fail-closed runtime schemas must exhaust their canonical types. defineExhaustiveShape<T>() infers concrete schemas before demanding exact input and output parity, rejecting missing and extra keys, narrowed unions, coercion, any at any nesting depth, and never during typecheck. src/entities/runtime-schema.ts composes the strict per-kind decoders used by storage, HTTP, format-corpus verification, and Kit draft validation. Unknown keys are accepted only inside extension or escrow bags whose contracts explicitly declare openness. Core logic cannot depend on UI, perform effects at import, or conceal authority in a singleton. Code throws Error objects or typed errors, and user-facing boundaries translate failures plainly.
- **H2 Tests** (`tests`)
  Bun is the test runner, and bun run test discovers every authored test under src and scripts. Bug fixes begin with a regression at the public boundary that reproduces the reported behavior before the repair. Same-format adapter fixtures must parse, serialize, and reparse without semantic loss, protecting the Round-Trip Law at the format boundary. Tests remain deterministic by avoiding live network access, uncontrolled clocks, and unseeded randomness. A nominally green result that still prints framework synchronization warnings is not clean verification and must not be represented as such.
- **H2 CLI** (`cli`)
  The target automation contract covers the non-interactive data commands convert, inspect, validate, label, formats, and version. JSON mode must emit exactly one JSON value to stdout, while human diagnostics go to stderr. Exit code 0 means success, 1 means invalid user input or invalid content, and 2 means an unexpected internal failure. help and the long-running ui server are outside this JSON contract. The section explicitly says implementation is incomplete: until all commands comply and command-matrix tests land, docs/reference/cli.md remains the authority for current syntax and behavior rather than this target being treated as shipped proof.
- **H2 Repository** (`repository`)
  Development requires Bun 1.3.14 or later; CI reads the exact .bun-version. Commit subjects describe the change and skip release is the only machine-read marker. Changes carry proof and never bypass hooks. Virtualization goes through the shared virtual-list module: VirtualRows for flat lists, VirtualShelf for card grids. Host code never keys on a theme id. Every data-part is a dotted id-keyed name on every scenes wing, tab and pop-out (scan:virtualizers, scan:theme-keys, scan:parts).
- **H2 Documentation** (`documentation`)
  Reference pages state built behavior only. Planned work belongs under specs or in a roadmap section that labels it as planned, preventing intent from masquerading as a shipped surface. Documentation changes in the same change as its source so contracts and user guidance stay aligned. Generated indexes and semantic summaries are navigation aids, not authority: they cannot transform an inaccurate source page into truth.
- **H2 Working method** (`working-method`)
  Before editing, inspect the implementation, its callers and tests, release configuration, documentation, and Git state, then name both the governing authority and user-visible outcome. Bugs and known contracts begin with an honest failing test at the nearest public boundary. A genuinely novel design may use a bounded spike until its contract is understood, but completion still requires behavioral proof. Repair the owning boundary rather than creating a parallel schema, registry, state authority, or compatibility path. When code and docs disagree, classify the claim as IMPLEMENT, REWRITE, DELETE, or RETAIN. Verification progresses through focused, adjacent, and complete supported checks, with warnings and skips reported rather than counted as clean. Substantial or high-risk work receives independent adversarial review; authors cannot approve their own semantic summaries, security claims, migrations, or release evidence.

### Format support matrix

- id: `FORMAT-SUPPORT`
- path: `docs/FORMAT-SUPPORT.md`
- Auto-generated format support matrix from the live adapter registry, grouped by canonical content kind, in the kind hub's order and names, with adapter id, output extensions, and human label. It documents CLI listing, inspection, conversion, and validation, with SillyTavern CCv3 as a portable format for targets that accept Character Card v3. The current registry has eleven character, nine lorebook, four persona, one pack, four preset, one Script Sheet, five regex-set, one quick-reply and one prompt-format adapter. The pack adapter, image-pack, writes a zip of pictures and sounds. Tools (kind tool), dice (kind dice), formulas (kind formula), trackers (kind tracker), skills (kind skill) and drawings (kind htmldoc) have no import or export format yet, and their sections say so. Notes explain content-sniff detection, fixture-proven same-format round trips, honest cross-format loss reporting, and sample locations. Because the page is generated, counts and rows change only with the registry. Native Bunzai Promptbook and Agent adapters now read and write canonical JSON; these preserve reference IDs rather than embedding linked pieces.

- **H2 How to use** (`how-to-use`)
  Documents the primary CLI entry points for format work: bun run bunzai formats to list adapters, bunzai inspect on a card path, bunzai convert with --to for a target such as sillytavern, and bunzai validate on card JSON. For targets accepting Character Card v3, the portable character format is SillyTavern CCv3 through the sillytavern adapter.
- **H2 Characters (11)** (`characters-11`)
  Eleven character adapters share the canonical character kind. Agnai, Backyard, RoleCall and Bunzai native write JSON. BYAF writes Backyard archives; Risu writes charx. Lumiverse writes JSON and charx. Pygmalion and SillyTavern write JSON or PNG. JanitorAI and Saucepan are export-only PNG targets using recorded importer mappings. Detection remains owned by each adapter, so export-only targets do not claim imported files.
- **H2 Lorebooks (9)** (`lorebooks-9`)
  Nine lorebook adapters. JanitorAI and Saucepan add export-only ST world info JSON targets. agnai-lorebook writes .json Agnai memory books. marinara-lorebook writes .json native .marinara.json export. novelai-lorebook writes .lorebook for NovelAI native export or json. risu-lorebook writes .json RisuAI native export. rolecall-lorebook writes .json RoleCall v1 export. rolecall-compendium writes .json RoleCall compendium export. sillytavern-lorebook writes .json SillyTavern world info worldbook. All are kind lorebook.
- **H2 Personas (4)** (`personas-4`)
  Four persona adapters, all writing .json. lumiverse-persona is a Lumiverse account object. marinara-persona carries theming and stat bars sealed. rolecall-persona is rcpersona or RC persona-card export. sillytavern-persona is a SillyTavern personas backup where the default persona is primary and the rest ride sealed.
- **H2 Packs (1)** (`packs-1`)
  Packs (1): the live matrix lists one pack adapter, image-pack, kind pack, which writes .zip files and is labeled Pack (zip of pictures and sounds). The row is derived from the registered adapter, so the section count, id, kind, written extension and label change only when the runtime registry changes, as for every section of this generated page.
- **H2 Presets (4)** (`presets-4`)
  Four preset adapters, all writing .json. lumiverse-preset imports a wrapper and exports as an ST flat preset. marinara-preset is Marinara-Engine marinara_preset export. rolecall-preset is RoleCall preset export with ST grammar plus macros, choice groups, and readme. sillytavern-preset is SillyTavern completion preset as flat json.

  Script Sheets (1): the live matrix lists one Script Sheet adapter, rolecall-script-sheet, kind scriptsheet, which writes .json files and is labeled RoleCall Script Sheet export (one instruction, with the blocks it sends). It is the only adapter registered for the scriptsheet kind, and its section sits after the presets in the kind hub's order, under the kind hub's plural name. The row is derived from the registered adapter, so the section count, id, kind, written extension and label change only when the runtime registry changes, as for every section of this generated page.

### Documentation

- id: `README`
- path: `docs/README.md`
- Complete documentation map for Bunzai's user guidance, contributor references, accepted decisions, future contracts, and generated support material. Guide lists task pages for setup, importing, converting, editing, exporting, and lorebooks, plus platform pages for Agnai, Backyard, Chub, Lumiverse, Marinara, NovelAI, Pygmalion, RisuAI, RoleCall, and SillyTavern. Reference points builders to the reference-tree contract, as-built architecture, canonical entity schemas, format coverage, cross-cutting concepts, the Studio and Kit UI, generated component catalog, and platform-native field research; Chub is represented through its guide, coverage matrix, and adapter source rather than a dedicated reference page. Decisions and planning separates accepted ADRs, the required vision/architecture/conventions spine, the detailed source map, roadmap exit criteria, and the live-registry FORMAT-SUPPORT matrix. It also distinguishes generated field tables, figure data, and media assets from browsable prose pages, and warns that specifications describe planned or load-bearing contracts without proving every feature ships.

- **H2 Guide** (`guide`)
  Guide is the task-oriented user path. Getting started covers installation, first-run setup, the Dock, and the first imported file. Importing explains bringing files into the Library and interpreting receipts. Converting covers moving a card between platform formats. Editing follows a piece onto the Workbench and through explicit draft saving. Exporting covers both the character editor's send-out flow and staged batch output. Lorebooks teaches entries, keywords, triggers, and placement. The platform directory then supplies one source-platform page for Agnai, Backyard, Chub, Lumiverse, Marinara, NovelAI, Pygmalion, RisuAI, RoleCall, and SillyTavern.
- **H2 Reference** (`reference`)
  Reference is the contributor-facing statement of shipping behavior. Its root explains the tree contract and field tables; architecture maps the canonical model, escrow, adapters, entity kinds, registry, and CLI. Entity pages define character, lorebook, pack, persona, preset, and regex schemas. Format pages pair the coverage matrix with family-specific behavior, while concept pages cover embedded character books, persona and regex editors, regex execution, safe rendering, and tags. The UI page covers the visual Studio and Kit, components is generated from source, and platform-native-fields records verified native research. Chub has no dedicated reference page yet, so its current format truth remains in the matrix and adapter folder.
- **H2 Decisions and planning** (`decisions-and-planning`)
  Decisions and planning explains which documents carry authority. Accepted ADRs live under docs/decisions and must be read in order with explicit supersession honored. The required spine is 01-VISION for product intent, 02-ARCHITECTURE for canonical structure, and 03-CONVENTIONS for implementation rules; reference/architecture is the detailed current source map. ROADMAP holds milestones and release exit criteria. FORMAT-SUPPORT is generated from the live adapter registry with bun run matrix and records per-field format coverage. Detailed specs remain contracts for planned or load-bearing areas, not automatic shipping evidence. Generated field tables, figure data, and media screenshots support other pages and are not standalone navigation destinations.

## decisions

### ADR-001: Runtime - Bun + TypeScript, Node-compatible core

- id: `decisions/ADR-001-runtime`
- path: `docs/decisions/ADR-001-runtime.md`
- Accepted ADR choosing TypeScript everywhere with Bun as the toolchain for dev runtime, bun test, bundling, and bun build --compile single-file executables as primary distribution. Core packages core, formats, lore, presets, macros, regexkit, and assembly must not use Bun-only APIs and must stay runnable under Node for npm publish and embedding. apps, agent, and ai may use Bun APIs where they matter for spawn, file IO speed, and compile. Why: one-file no-install distribution is the top goal; TypeScript matches VAUDEVILLE extraction surface and zod dual use as validation and docs; Node-compat core keeps npm and embedding open and de-risks Bun stalls. Consequences: CI runs core package tests under both Bun and Node; a lint rule bans bun star imports inside packages except an allowlist of ai and agent. Status is accepted.

- **H2 Decision** (`decision`)
  TypeScript is used everywhere. Bun is the toolchain for development runtime, test runner via bun test, bundler, and bun build --compile for single-file executables as primary distribution. Core packages named core, formats, lore, presets, macros, regexkit, and assembly must not use Bun-only APIs so they remain runnable under Node for npm publish and embedding by other tools. apps plus agent and ai may use Bun APIs where they matter for spawn, file IO speed, and compile.
- **H2 Why** (`why`)
  A single-file executable is the stated distribution goal, and bun build --compile is the direct path across the supported operating systems. TypeScript matches the VAUDEVILLE extraction surface, while zod schemas serve as validation and documentation. A Node-compatible core keeps npm publishing and embedding available and limits the impact if Bun stalls.
- **H2 Consequences** (`consequences`)
  CI must run core package tests under both Bun and Node so Node-compat claims stay proven. A lint rule bans bun colon star imports inside packages except an allowlist covering the ai and agent packages, which are permitted to use Bun-only APIs where they matter.

### ADR-002: License, AGPL-3.0-or-later

- id: `decisions/ADR-002-license`
- path: `docs/decisions/ADR-002-license.md`
- Accepted ADR selecting AGPL-3.0-or-later for the whole repo to keep code genuinely open while preventing closed commercial exploitation. Context forced the choice because openness and anti-enclosure pull against each other under a permissive license. Decision allows the copyright holder to sell commercial licenses to parties who cannot comply with AGPL, following Grafana, Qt, and pre-SSPL MongoDB dual-licensing. Why AGPL fits: OSI-approved open source without source-available stigma; forks and hosted services must publish complete source under same terms so commercial actors stay away or negotiate; copyright holder is not bound by their own license for other closed projects. Rejected PolyForm-NC FSL BUSL as source-available, MIT Apache as allowing closed forks, and MIT core plus AGPL app as weaker where it matters most though revisitable. Consequences: LICENSE verbatim AGPL text, LICENSING.md plain explanation and commercial contact, license audit fails build on copyleft restricted deps, no CLA is a live constraint so outside contributions cannot merge without forfeiting commercial dual-license option, no per-file SPDX headers.

- **H2 Context** (`context`)
  The project needed a license that keeps the code genuinely open and usable by anyone while preventing a third party from closing it up and selling it. Those two goals pull against each other under a permissive license, which forced a deliberate decision rather than a default choice.
- **H2 Decision** (`decision`)
  AGPL-3.0-or-later applies to the whole repository. The copyright holder retains the right to sell commercial licenses to anyone who cannot or will not comply with the AGPL. This dual-licensing model is the same pattern Grafana, Qt, and pre-SSPL MongoDB used for open core plus commercial terms.
- **H2 Why AGPL fits** (`why-agpl-fits`)
  AGPL is a real OSI-approved open-source license without source-available stigma. It prevents closed commercial exploitation in practice because any fork or hosted service must publish its complete source under the same terms, which commercial actors rarely accept; they either stay away or negotiate a separate license. The copyright holder is not bound by their own license, so the same code can still be used in their other closed projects.
- **H2 Rejected alternatives** (`rejected-alternatives`)
  PolyForm-NC, FSL, and BUSL are closer to the literal anti-commercial requirement but are source-available rather than open source and invite community suspicion this choice exists to avoid. MIT and Apache allow closed commercial forks outright. MIT core plus AGPL app is a cleaner engine adoption story but weakens the guarantee where it matters most; revisitable per-package later because relicensing toward MIT stays open to the copyright holder while the reverse is not.
- **H2 Consequences** (`consequences`)
  LICENSE carries verbatim AGPL-3.0 text; LICENSING.md explains it in plain terms and carries the commercial contact. bun run license:audit fails the build if a copyleft or restricted dependency is introduced. No CLA is in place and this is a live constraint: dual licensing depends on the copyright holder owning all code; the first merged outside contribution without a contributor agreement ends that option. Until a CLA exists, outside contributions cannot be merged without forfeiting commercial licensing. Per-file SPDX headers were considered and are not adopted; the repo-level LICENSE governs.

### ADR-003: Distribution - single executable + self-updater; installer later

- id: `decisions/ADR-003-distribution`
- path: `docs/decisions/ADR-003-distribution.md`
- Accepted distribution design decision. The intended v0.1 primary path is single-file executables per OS built by bun build --compile and attached to GitHub Releases; the ADR describes plans, not proof that release artifacts, signing, checksums, or updates have shipped. It also plans a self-updater with SHA-256 verification against a minisign-signed checksum file, atomic replacement, and passive notices only; later Tauri installers and package-manager options; and secondary npm packages and npx. Why: one executable simplifies setup without requiring separate Node or Git installs; GitHub Releases offers bandwidth and reaches the audience. Consequences describe a planned release matrix, checksum/signature publication, unsigned Windows SmartScreen warnings, provenance and integrity guidance, and accepted binary size.

- **H2 Decision** (`decision`)
  The intended v0.1 primary distribution is a single-file executable for each listed OS and architecture, built with bun build --compile and attached to GitHub Releases. Planned vaud upgrade behavior checks a downloaded binary against SHA-256 values in a minisign-signed checksum file before atomic replacement; notices stay passive and installs require consent. Tauri installers and package managers are later options; npm packages and npx remain secondary. This is an accepted design, not evidence those artifacts or mechanisms have shipped.
- **H2 Why** (`why`)
  A single executable simplifies setup by avoiding separate Node and Git installation. The accepted plan uses bun build --compile to make that path practical across the named operating systems. GitHub Releases offers a distribution channel with bandwidth and reaches the intended audience; this decision does not claim that binaries have already shipped.
- **H2 Consequences** (`consequences`)
  The ADR plans a per-tag binary matrix with checksums and signatures; these are planned release requirements, not confirmation of published artifacts. Unsigned Windows executables may trigger SmartScreen. Distribution guidance should disclose signing status and show users how to check provenance and integrity, without presenting a blanket bypass as safe. The expected 50–90 MB size from the embedded Bun runtime is accepted and should be documented.

### ADR-005: Canonical superset model + escrow envelope

- id: `decisions/ADR-005-canonical-model`
- path: `docs/decisions/ADR-005-canonical-model.md`
- Accepted ADR establishing Bunzai's canonical superset and escrow model. Its schema-authority note records that ADR-011 supersedes the original Zod-under-packages/core wording: current domain contracts are handwritten interfaces in src/entities/<kind>/schema.ts paired with exhaustive adjacent runtime decoders. The Decision retains one canonical model per content type, carries fields without a canonical home in origin-keyed escrow, merges escrow for semantically identical same-format round trips, and requires honest reports for cross-format loss or recovery through tolerated extension fields. Why not "just use chara_card_v3 internally" explains that V3 covers cards only and cannot represent all RoleCall, Risu, Backyard, lorebook, preset, or persona meaning. Precedents in the author's own earlier code identifies RoleCall unsupportedFields and origin plus VAUDEVILLE normalizers as earlier forms of the pattern. Consequences requires codec capability tables to drive conversion reporting and the generated support matrix, while changes to canonical domain contracts demand a migration note, fixture sweep, and explicit design review. ADR-005's superset, escrow envelope, and Round-Trip Law remain accepted even though ADR-011 now owns runtime schema authority.

- **H2 Decision** (`decision`)
  The decision text defines one canonical superset per content type and an origin-keyed escrow envelope for fields without a canonical home. Its parse and serialize equations require reports at both boundaries. Same-format serialization merges escrow so the result remains semantically identical under the Round-Trip Law. Cross-format serialization expresses supported meaning, reports escrowed or dropped fields, and preserves escrow where the target permits extension fields, allowing later recovery. The section's historical statement places Zod schemas in packages/core, but the document's schema-authority note explicitly supersedes that detail through ADR-011. Current authority is handwritten domain interfaces under src/entities/<kind>/schema.ts paired with exhaustive runtime decoders; the superset and escrow behavior itself is unchanged.
- **H2 Why not "just use chara_card_v3 internally"** (`why-not-just-use-chara_card_v3-internally`)
  V3 is card-only and still lossy against RoleCall details palette sprites and depth-injections, Risu extensions, and Backyard fields. Lorebooks, presets, and personas need models V3 does not define. A superset with escrow means the project never argues with a format author about whose fields matter.
- **H2 Precedents in the author's own earlier code** (`precedents-in-the-authors-own-earlier-code`)
  RoleCall LorebookEntry unsupportedFields and origin fields are this escrow pattern in miniature. VAUDEVILLE normalizers already unify V1 V2 V3 rcpersona and RoleOut into a common shape. This ADR promotes that established habit into a system-wide law rather than inventing a new approach.
- **H2 Consequences** (`consequences`)
  Every codec ships a capabilities table mapping canonical field to native, escrow, or dropped that powers honest conversion reports and the docs format-support matrix which is auto-generated and never hand-maintained. core schema changes are the most expensive changes in the repo: they require a migration note, a fixture sweep, and explicit design review.

### ADR-006: AI - BYOK + local, and an agent built for weak models

- id: `decisions/ADR-006-ai-and-agent`
- path: `docs/decisions/ADR-006-ai-and-agent.md`
- Accepted ADR for AI posture and weak-model agent design. BYOK first with Anthropic, OpenRouter, OpenAI, Gemini, and any OpenAI-compatible endpoint covering Ollama LM Studio koboldcpp for local; no Vaudeville-operated inference, no account, no proxy; keys in local vault OS keychain or encrypted file. AI is optional: Converter, inspection, validation, deterministic Doctor checks, and project management work with zero keys; first key ask is at first AI moment never at install. Agent must survive weak models using Orison lessons: tiny always-on tools with deferred schemas, resource verbs over vaud URIs, prompted XML tool-calling fallback one tool per iteration, spill store for big results, explicit samplers, staged edits with validate-before-commit never writing user files directly. Model roles are user-mappable per interview treatment test-stage and bulk audits with a sane single-model default. Eval harness at M2 before agent feature work with golden tasks replayable transcripts and model matrix requiring success-rate and tokens-per-task movement. Why: local-first BYOK matches privacy pitch and audience that owns keys; weak-model constraints also make frontier behavior cheaper and more reliable.

- **H2 Decision** (`decision`)
  BYOK first: Anthropic, OpenRouter, OpenAI, Gemini, plus any OpenAI-compatible endpoint for Ollama LM Studio koboldcpp local; no Vaudeville-operated inference account or proxy; keys in OS keychain or encrypted file. AI is optional so Converter inspection validation deterministic Doctor and project management need zero keys; first key ask is at first AI moment never install. Agent must survive weak models via tiny always-on tools with deferred schemas, resource verbs ls read write search over vaud URIs, prompted XML fallback with one tool per iteration, spill store to disk handles, explicit samplers with max_tokens room for tool args, and staged edits validate-before-commit never writing user files directly. Model roles are user-mappable across interview treatment test-stage and bulk audits with a sane single-model default. Eval harness at M2 before agent feature work uses golden tasks, replayable transcripts, and a model matrix; agent changes must move success-rate and tokens-per-task numbers.
- **H2 Why** (`why`)
  Local-first plus BYOK is the only stance consistent with the privacy pitch and with an audience that already owns keys and local rigs. Weak-model constraints are not charity: they make frontier-model behavior cheaper and more reliable too, which is Orison's measured lesson.

### ADR-007: Renderer policy (shell vanilla, per-app freedom, chat surfaces re-evaluated at spec time)

- id: `decisions/ADR-007-renderer-policy`
- path: `docs/decisions/ADR-007-renderer-policy.md`
- Accepted ADR on renderer policy dated 2026-07-05. Context: shell and then-current surfaces were vanilla TS plus DOM with five runtime dependencies and one compiled exe per ADR-001 and ADR-003, while roadmap chat surfaces M4 Table Read and agent packages are classically React-shaped. Two facts prevent a stack war: Table Read and agent engines are UI-agnostic with zero rendering code and one event protocol; the app contract manifest plus mount is renderer-agnostic so an app folder may bundle its own library. Decision: shell and editor shelf-class surfaces stay vanilla TS plus DOM; every app folder has renderer freedom; when Table Read and agent chat surfaces reach implementation their specs must include a renderer assessment with React on the table against streaming updates, virtualized long lists, deep interactive state webs, and component-ecosystem needs; if two or more criteria hold React is the default for that surface otherwise vanilla; engines never acquire a renderer dependency. Consequences: no restart of prior layers; two rendering idioms may coexist bounded by folders-as-schema; this is the standing answer that React is decided per-surface at spec time. Note ADR-008 later supersedes the deferral of chat-surface choice while keeping the engine rule.

- **H2 Context** (`context`)
  Shell and then-current Library Workbench Press Settings setup surfaces were hand-built vanilla TS plus DOM with five runtime dependencies and one compiled exe. Roadmap also promises chat-shaped surfaces: M4 Table Read conversational interview and agent system packages agent REPL test chat with Studio later at M6. Chat runtimes are classically React-shaped for streaming state and live lists, and comparable apps RoleCall Marinara Lumiverse frontend render chat with React 19. Two facts avoid a stack war: Table Read spec locks the engine as UI-agnostic with zero rendering code and one event protocol shared by CLI and Studio; agent packages follow the same rule so studio may not own private engine capabilities; renderer choice never touches engine design. App contract of manifest plus mount is renderer-agnostic; an app folder may bundle its own rendering library and the shell neither knows nor cares.
- **H2 Decision** (`decision`)
  Shell and editor shelf-class surfaces stay vanilla TS plus DOM because form and card complexity does not justify a framework runtime. Every app folder has renderer freedom: bundling a framework inside one app is an add not a rewrite and needs no shell change. When Table Read and agent chat surfaces reach implementation their specs must include a renderer assessment against sustained streaming updates, long virtualized lists, deep interactive state webs, and concrete component-ecosystem needs, with React explicitly on the table. If two or more criteria hold, default for that surface is React with Bun-native TSX baked into the exe; otherwise vanilla, with the event protocol keeping a later swap cheap. Engines never acquire a renderer dependency under any outcome.
- **H2 Consequences** (`consequences`)
  No restart scenario exists: converter, canonical model, engines, shell, editors, and tokens CSS serve both renderer outcomes unchanged. The repo may eventually carry two rendering idioms isolated per app folder; that cost is accepted and bounded by folders-as-schema. This ADR is the standing answer to whether Studio should be React: the question is only ever asked per-surface with written criteria at the moment the surface is specced.

### ADR-008: React UI layer for structural rendering safety

- id: `decisions/ADR-008-react-ui`
- path: `docs/decisions/ADR-008-react-ui.md`
- Accepted ADR dated 2026-07-05 that supersedes ADR-007's deferral of chat-surface renderer choice while carrying forward and hardening the engine rule. Context: Bunzai renders untrusted card and extension text, so direct DOM construction makes HTML-sink safety depend on caller convention; React escapes text by default and repository gates ban explicit escape hatches. Roadmap chat and agent surfaces plus agent-context plans require serializable UI state; closure-held vanilla DOM is agent-opaque while store-driven React matches comparable production apps. Decision: UI layer is React 19 plus TSX transpiled natively by Bun with no Vite or Next, bundled into the same single exe; Zustand for shell store plus one store per app; no URL router; CSS Modules over tokens with no Tailwind; whole UI converts to one idiom; app contract v2 is React component export plus manifest including agentSurface descriptor; engine rule forbids renderer imports in converter canonical model engines and star-core files; react react-dom and zustand are direct dependencies covered by license audit while new dependencies follow normal review without a special commit trailer; dangerouslySetInnerHTML and raw HTML sinks are banned outside an allowlist. Consequences: rewrite bounded by pure cores as spec; ordinary text is escaped by default and the sink gate enforces escape-hatch policy; established tooling lowers maintenance cost; framework churn risk accepted with engine immune.

- **H2 Context** (`context`)
  Two constraints crystallized after ADR-007. Bunzai renders untrusted card and extension text, so direct DOM construction makes HTML-sink safety depend on every caller; React escapes text by default and gates ban explicit escape hatches. Roadmap chat and agent surfaces plus per-surface context require serializable UI state; closure-held vanilla DOM is agent-opaque while store-driven React matches comparable production applications.
- **H2 Decision** (`decision`)
  UI layer is React 19 plus TSX transpiled natively by Bun without Vite or Next and bundled into the same single exe. State is Zustand with one shell store plus one store per app surface; navigation is state-driven with no URL router. Styling is CSS Modules over the existing token system with no Tailwind. The UI converts whole shell and every app folder to one idiom; until conversion lands, vanilla surfaces remain the behavioral spec. App contract v2 is a React component export plus manifest including an agentSurface descriptor of state selector and offered actions so every surface is agent-readable by construction. Engine rule unchanged and hardened: converter, canonical model, engines, and every star-core.ts stay pure framework-less TS; a renderer import there is a guardrail violation. React, react-dom, and zustand are direct dependencies covered by license audit; new dependencies follow normal review without a special commit trailer. dangerouslySetInnerHTML and raw HTML-injection sinks are banned by repo gates outside an explicit sanctioned allowlist.
- **H2 Consequences** (`consequences`)
  Editor slice 1 and the current shell are rebuilt in React during conversion; their behavior and pure tested cores are the spec so the rewrite is bounded. Ordinary text rendering is escaped by default, while repository gates separately enforce the raw-HTML escape-hatch ban. Established tooling and contributor familiarity reduce maintenance cost. React version churn is accepted and bounded because the engine remains isolated.

### ADR-009: Distinct origin for test-bench execution

- id: `decisions/ADR-009-sandbox-origin`
- path: `docs/decisions/ADR-009-sandbox-origin.md`
- Accepted-in-design ADR for a distinct origin for test-bench execution; packaged-host proof remains BLOCKED as of 2026-07-09. Context: Risu Test Bench runs untrusted Lua and regex previews in dedicated Workers with resource ceilings; historically workers shared the UI loopback origin which is only an availability boundary not an origin boundary. Decision chooses a second loopback listener on an ephemeral port as a sandbox-only origin serving only worker.js, regex-worker.js, and glue.wasm with CORS for the UI origin, never exposing /api, studio paths, session-token HTML, arbitrary static, or secret source maps. UI injects vaude-sandbox-origin meta; API token stays only in vaude-session and trusted apiFetch. Lua and regex wires are value-only protocols with size and rule caps. Rejected same-path sandbox, allow-same-origin iframe, CORS-only, and deferred helper process. Measured results automate unit allowlist protocol and packaged asset build but not packaged WebView2 probes. Consequences: HTTP-layer origin separation is implementable; do not claim full sandbox isolation in release notes until WebView2 matrix is green; Plans 005 and 016 remain load-bearing. STOP honesty: Plan 017 full Done is BLOCKED until packaged measurements exist. Addendum 2026-10-12: a scene's switched-on regex sets run through the same worker in batches; the turn loop waits for the page and never runs a rule, and AGENTS rule 3 says so.

- **H2 Context** (`context`)
  Risu Test Bench runs untrusted Lua in a dedicated Worker with Plan 016 resource ceilings for timeout, heap, host-state, and wire size. Regex previews run in a dedicated terminable Worker with a 750 ms wall-clock ceiling. Plan 005 authenticates the loopback API with a per-launch bearer X-Vaude-Token and meta vaude-session. Historically the worker module was served from the same loopback origin as the UI and /api. A same-origin Worker is an availability boundary (killable thread), not an origin boundary. If a future engine bundler WebView or bridge bug exposes web capabilities, same-origin placement increases blast radius for same-origin APIs, storage, or credentials. This is defense-in-depth architecture, not evidence of a current same-origin exploit.
- **H2 Packaged-host acceptance** (`packaged-host-acceptance`)
  Before a public Windows release, check the freshly built dist/Vaude.exe not the development server: open Workshop Test Bench, run return 42, and run a Regex Bench preview. Both must finish without a worker-load error. Record build version and result in release notes. Automated suite already proves distinct loopback authority, allowlist CSP CORS, API denial, token absence, and protocol; this acceptance specifically covers installed WebView2 runtime.
- **H2 Threat model (in scope)** (`threat-model-in-scope`)
  In-scope assets if sandbox shares the app origin: per-launch API token leak into script state worker globals messages logs or URLs; mutating /api routes via cross-origin-less fetch with ambient authority; studio filesystem write or delete through authenticated routes; trusted cookies and localStorage shared with the worker. Out of scope: arbitrary untrusted native-code isolation, giving Lua DOM fetch or fs access, and auto-running scripts on import.
- **H2 Decision** (`decision`)
  Chosen boundary is a second loopback listener where distinct port equals distinct origin. Primary UI API server stays on 127.0.0.1 configured port with Host Origin token gating from Plan 005. Sandbox-only server binds 127.0.0.1 on an ephemeral port and serves only GET sandbox worker.js, regex-worker.js, and glue.wasm plus CORS preflight for the UI origin. Sandbox host must not expose /api, studio paths, index HTML with session token, arbitrary static files, or source maps with secrets. UI HTML injects meta vaude-sandbox-origin which is public not a secret; API token stays only in meta vaude-session and trusted apiFetch. Browser UI loads workers from sandbox origin when meta is present; Bun tests keep module-relative worker paths. Lua wire protocol is a versioned value-only tagged union with allowlisted keys, Plan 016 size budgets, no token fields, no functions handles or ports except the Worker channel. Regex worker accepts values only and rejects oversized input, more than 500 rules, and malformed rows or options before the core engine. Nested sections reject weaker isolation alternatives and document origin alias fail-closed behavior.
  - **H3 Rejected alternatives** (`rejected-alternatives`)
    Reject same-path sandbox as same origin; reject iframe allow-same-origin; reject CORS alone as non-auth. Defer helper child process until measured need for packaging cost.
  - **H3 Origin / alias notes** (`origin-alias-notes`)
    Distinct 127.0.0.1 ports are distinct origins. Host checks fail closed on aliases. Sandbox origin never authorizes API calls without token and correct Origin.
- **H2 Measured results (this environment)** (`measured-results-this-environment`)
  Unit checks automated: sandbox handler allowlist serves only the two workers plus wasm and returns 404 for /api and traversal; value protocol rejects wrong version unknown type secret keys and oversized payloads; worker run without token in messages via protocol encode parse. Packaged asset build is automated by bun run scripts/build-desktop.ts. Not run or not measured in this implementation pass: packaged WebView2 origin probe on Windows, self.crossOriginIsolated and storage visibility in WebView, and credentialed API fetch from a sandbox-origin worker in the packaged host.
- **H2 Rollback / packaging** (`rollback-packaging`)
  startUi starts both listeners and stop tears both down. Packaged exe uses the same dual-listen path; sandbox serves both packaged worker bundles plus wasm. If sandbox listen fails, UI can fall back to the matching same-origin worker path on the primary server as degraded mode not claimed isolated; prefer fail-visible logs over silent false isolation. Re-run the origin probe when WebView, bundler, server, or packaging dependencies change.
- **H2 Consequences** (`consequences`)
  Real origin separation is implementable and tested at the HTTP handler layer in Bun. Packaged-host proof remains BLOCKED until a WebView2 smoke matrix records distinct authority, no shared storage or token, API denial from the sandbox origin, and CSP probes. Do not claim full sandbox isolation in release notes until that matrix is green. Plans 005 token and 016 quotas remain load-bearing layers; origin isolation is additive.
- **H2 Addendum 2026-10-12: a scene's regex** (`addendum-2026-10-12-a-scenes-regex`)
  A scene's display and prompt regex (ChatStore rule 42) is the first use of the regex worker outside the Regex Bench and adds no execution path. The page posts the same value-only requests, now also in batches through request.ts, to the same worker on the same isolated origin. The turn loop lives in the Bun process and never runs a rule; it holds its request and waits for the page's answer. Choosing the sets for a scene is the invocation. AGENTS rule 3 now says the same: imported scripts run only inside the worker, which serves the test benches and a scene's sets; nothing outside it executes them; a worker that cannot run fails closed.
- **H2 STOP / honesty** (`stop-honesty`)
  This ADR deliberately does not assert that the shipped WebView collapses authorities safely. Until packaged measurements exist, Plan 017 status is BLOCKED for full Done criteria while the code path of sandbox host protocol and token absence ships as defense-in-depth scaffolding.

### ADR-010: Shared semantic content capabilities

- id: `decisions/ADR-010-content-capabilities`
- path: `docs/decisions/ADR-010-content-capabilities.md`
- Accepted ADR dated 2026-07-25 for shared semantic content capabilities. Context: Kit will eventually need more than one hundred content-editing operations; advertising every schema on every model request wastes context and hurts tool selection; implementing the same mutation separately in Kit and Workbench would allow drift. Filesystem is already the registry for formats and UI extensions, so content edits need the same drop-in property while canonical validation and escrow preservation remain mandatory. Decision: pure semantic operations live under src/entities kind capabilities; platform-specific operations may live under src/formats platform capabilities; one immutable catalog validates stable dotted IDs and supports deterministic metadata search; Kit keeps a complete runtime registry but exposes only direct tools and up to five successfully discovered deferred capabilities to the model; browser uses a generated import seam from the same folders never a second hand-maintained catalog; a capability produces a validated preview and never writes; Kit composes previews into a session-local draft while apply stale-revision checks persistence and verification are separate runtime responsibilities; Web UI imports the same pure operations and keeps presentation-only state locally. Consequences: add operations via drop-in and proof; small task-relevant tool sets; shared mutation meaning; capability IDs become compatibility-sensitive once shipped; live apply rejects stale drafts and reports only verified post-save receipts as applied.

- **H2 Context** (`context`)
  Kit will eventually need more than one hundred content-editing operations. Advertising every schema on every model request wastes context and makes tool selection less reliable. Implementing the same mutation separately in Kit and the Workbench would also allow their behavior to drift. The filesystem is already Bunzai's registry for formats and UI extensions. Content edits need the same drop-in property, while canonical validation and escrow preservation remain mandatory.
- **H2 Decision** (`decision`)
  Pure semantic operations live under src/entities kind capabilities folders. Platform-specific operations may live under src/formats platform capabilities. One immutable catalog validates stable dotted IDs and supports deterministic metadata search. Kit keeps a complete runtime registry but exposes only direct tools and up to five successfully discovered deferred capabilities to the model. The browser uses a generated import seam from the same folders; the generated file is never a second hand-maintained catalog. A capability produces a validated preview and never writes. Kit composes previews into a session-local draft; applying, stale-revision checks, persistence, and verification are separate runtime responsibilities. The Web UI imports the same pure operations and keeps presentation-only state such as focus and undo locally.
- **H2 Consequences** (`consequences`)
  Adding an operation means adding a capability drop-in and proof, not editing a central registry. Models receive small task-relevant typed tool sets instead of one huge static catalog. Kit and the Workbench can share mutation meaning without sharing surface state. Capability IDs become compatibility-sensitive once shipped. The live apply boundary rejects stale drafts and reports only a verified post-save receipt as applied.

### ADR-011: Exhaustive runtime schemas at canonical boundaries

- id: `decisions/ADR-011-runtime-boundary-schemas`
- path: `docs/decisions/ADR-011-runtime-boundary-schemas.md`
- Accepted on 2026-07-26, ADR-011 makes exhaustive runtime decoding the canonical boundary contract while preserving handwritten domain interfaces. TypeScript alone cannot validate JSON from disk, HTTP, adapters, or Kit drafts, and the former central decoder accepted only a required floor. The decision adds one adjacent runtime decoder per content kind and routes every decoded object through defineExhaustiveShape<T>(), which infers concrete Zod schemas before demanding exact domain-compatible inputs and outputs. Missing or extra keys, narrowed unions, coercion, any at any nesting depth, and never fail typecheck. Six strict bodies compose the public discriminated union; unknown same-version keys fail outside declared open record bags; profiles validate as kind-matched shallow partial bodies. Adapter-corpus tests, fully populated fixtures, malformed-family matrices, and storage, HTTP, Kit validation, and final-apply tests prove adoption. Strictness forces explicit schema evolution while leaving declared escrow and extension bags lossless and inert. ADR-005's canonical superset, escrow envelope, and Round-Trip Law remain in force.

- **H2 Context** (`context`)
  Canonical entities use rich handwritten TypeScript interfaces under src/entities/<kind>/schema.ts, but TypeScript types disappear at runtime and cannot validate JSON arriving from storage, HTTP, format adapters, or Kit drafts. The former central decoder checked the common envelope and only a required subset of each entity body. Because loose objects did not exhaust optional field families, a parse could succeed while carrying values that contradicted the domain interface into durable storage or editing logic. Replacing every interface with a schema-inferred type would cause broad disruption and still would not prove that every producer and consumer crosses one shared validation boundary. The problem is therefore boundary completeness and adoption, not merely the syntax used to declare types.
- **H2 Decision** (`decision`)
  Handwritten domain interfaces remain the internal semantic contracts. Each content kind owns an adjacent decoder at src/entities/<kind>/runtime-schema.ts. Every decoded object uses defineExhaustiveShape<T>(), which infers each concrete schema before requiring exact domain-compatible input and output types. Missing and extra keys, narrowed unions, coercion, any at any nesting depth, and never fail typecheck. src/entities/runtime-schema.ts composes six strict body decoders into the public discriminated canonical union. Canonical envelopes, bodies, profiles, and authored nested objects reject unknown current-version keys outside explicitly declared open carriers. Profiles use their kind's shallow partial body decoder. Adapter-corpus tests, fully populated per-kind fixtures, malformed-family matrices, and public-boundary tests prove adoption by format output, storage, HTTP save, Kit draft validation, and final Kit apply.
- **H2 Consequences** (`consequences`)
  A successful canonical parse now proves the complete known shape rather than a required floor. Interface and decoder drift, including narrowed unions and coercing inputs, becomes a compile-time error, while malformed external values fail at one shared runtime boundary with bounded messages. Storage, HTTP, adapters, and Kit no longer need competing ad hoc validators. Adding a canonical field requires the interface, adjacent decoder, positive fixture, and malformed-value regression to change together. Rejecting unknown same-version keys makes evolution deliberate. Declared escrow, metadata, extension, and other record bags remain lossless and inert. ADR-005's canonical superset, escrow envelope, and Round-Trip Law remain accepted.

### ADR-012: Macro evaluation is a closed-world interpreter, not a second script sandbox

- id: `decisions/ADR-012-macro-evaluation-boundary`
- path: `docs/decisions/ADR-012-macro-evaluation-boundary.md`
- Accepted on 2026-08-23, ADR-012 decides that macro evaluation is a closed-world interpreter rather than a second script sandbox. Bunzai cannot say whether a preset macro survives conversion because support.ts is name-level only: a missing name reliably dies, a present name proves nothing, and random with two arguments means different things on SillyTavern and RoleCall. Closing that gap needs the tokenizer, parser and tree-walking evaluator the macro engine spec designs, which collides with project rule 3 naming macros alongside Lua and regex as sealed payloads. The ADR narrows rule 3 in writing to Lua and regex, which keep ADR-009 isolation, and permits an in-process macro interpreter under bounded depth, output and steps, with no host surface, seeded randomness and clock, and unknown names degrading to literal text. Conversion still never evaluates: the Round-Trip Law boundary is promoted from spec text to accepted decision. Handlers are data-only, enforced by a registry test. A 2026-09-26 amendment adds chat display as a fourth caller. Accepted ADR-015 adds a fifth: brokered macros.preview from a user-invoked, manifest-declared command, with per-request confirmation, read-only execution, regex disabled and no persistence. Import, save, codec, automatic hook and background paths remain excluded.

- **H2 Context** (`context`)
  Context establishes the question Bunzai cannot answer today: whether a preset macro still works after conversion to another platform format. support.ts is name-level only by its own header, so a name missing from the target engine reliably dies while a name present proves nothing about syntax or meaning, and random with two arguments picks from a list on SillyTavern but is a numeric range on RoleCall. Closing that gap requires the tokenizer, parser and tree-walking evaluator that the macro engine spec already designs. That collides with project rule 3, which names macros alongside Lua and regex as sealed payloads, permits execution only in the user-invoked test benches on the ADR-009 sandbox origin, and forbids adding another execution path. Read widely the rule forbids the very engine the specs describe; read narrowly it governs only imported script payloads. The rule cannot be reinterpreted in a code comment by whoever implements the evaluator first, so it needs a decision.
- **H2 The distinction this ADR draws** (`the-distinction-this-adr-draws`)
  The distinction separates two hazards that rule 3 currently covers in one sentence. Sealed scripts are code in a general-purpose language: Lua is Turing-complete, regex carries catastrophic backtracking, both would reach an arbitrary host surface without isolation, and their failure modes are escape, exfiltration and hang. Macro templates are text with token substitutions whose expressive power is limited to whatever a registered handler does, whose reachable host surface is none because no handler touches filesystem, network or process, and whose failure modes are wrong text and unbounded expansion. A Lua interpreter runs whatever the payload says; a macro evaluator runs only handlers Bunzai wrote, selected by name from a closed registry, with an unrecognized name emitted as literal text. Applying one mitigation to both is not conservatism but a category error that would place a pure text-substitution pass behind a cross-origin worker for no security gain.
- **H2 Decision** (`decision`)
  The decision narrows rule 3 in writing to sealed Lua and regex payloads, which retain ADR-009 isolation, while permitting the in-process closed-world macro interpreter under the specified bounds. Conversion never evaluates macro text; the codec rule against evaluation during parse or serialize is accepted as a contract. The permitted caller list now has five entries: the user-invoked test bench, transfer compatibility report, future prompt assembly, chat display, and the bounded extension macros.preview caller accepted by ADR-015. The new caller must originate in an explicitly invoked manifest command and receive fresh host confirmation for each request; it evaluates once with read-only state and regex disabled. Other automatic extension activations remain excluded. Macro handlers remain data-only, with filesystem, network, process, timer and dynamic-code operations prohibited and enforced by a registry test.
- **H2 Constraints on the evaluator** (`constraints-on-the-evaluator`)
  Constraints on the evaluator are acceptance criteria rather than aspirations, and an implementation missing any of them does not satisfy the ADR. Recursion is bounded at depth one hundred, with a runaway subtree returning empty rather than throwing or hanging. Output is bounded by a hard ceiling on expanded characters enforced during evaluation rather than checked afterward, because expansion bombs are the realistic denial vector for a substitution engine and depth alone does not stop them. Steps are bounded by a node-evaluation budget so a wide template cannot substitute for a deep one. There is no ambient clock or randomness by default: volatile macros read a seed and a caller-supplied clock, since a test that cannot pin them cannot characterize them. The readOnly context makes side-effect macros no-ops. Unknown names remain literal and never throw or abort, while returned diagnostics may still name them as unresolved. MacroContext carries values only.
- **H2 Consequences** (`consequences`)
  Consequences are stated in both directions. The macro program becomes schedulable rather than blocked on an unresolved rule. The sandbox directory stays exactly as it is, gaining no capability and losing none. The security posture that actually matters is unchanged, because nothing imported from another platform is ever executed as code, at import or at conversion. A reviewer gains a specific checklist to hold an implementation against instead of arguing about whether rule 3 applies to macros. If the ADR is rejected the consequence is concrete and deliberately stated rather than left implicit: Bunzai can never prove macro behavior and can only compare macro names, so the transfer report stays permanently at the name-level honesty it has today.
- **H2 Amendment, 2026-09-26: chat display** (`amendment-2026-09-26-chat-display`)
  Chat shows messages as RoleCall's useDisplayMessages does: every message, the greeting included, runs through the full evaluator each time it is shown, so every macro the engine knows resolves in the bubble. It resolves against the chat's frozen cast rather than the live card, because the chat-store spec's rule 10 wins over RoleCall there. Volatile macros such as random, pick, and roll re-roll because the display caller passes a fresh seed per showing and each message holding a macro takes the next seed, keeping randomness caller-supplied. Variable writes run on a working copy with readOnly off and are dropped; prompt assembly is what will save variables, and until chats have them display starts from an empty copy. The stored message stays the authored text.
- **H2 What this ADR does not decide** (`what-this-adr-does-not-decide`)
  What this ADR does not decide is kept explicit so its scope cannot drift in later reading. It does not decide whether the Risu bracket CBS dialect or Agnai named-slot templates get modeled at all, because that is a scope question rather than a safety one and it is blocked on source availability rather than on this evaluation boundary. It also does not decide whether the evaluator is ever exposed to the model as a callable tool: a user-invoked test bench and a model-callable evaluation tool are separate exposure decisions, and only the former is contemplated here.

### ADR-013: The scene's wings and tabs get slots in the one slot registry

- id: `decisions/ADR-013-wing-slots`
- path: `docs/decisions/ADR-013-wing-slots.md`
- Proposed on 2026-10-12 and awaiting review, ADR-013 gives the scene's nine wings, and the tabs each one opens, slots in the one slot registry that theme packages already use. Until now a theme could restyle the Control Booth, Cast, Prompting, World, Final Cut, Props, AI, Stage and Context wings through named parts but could not add an image or a line of text to any of them, and the registry and permission audit recorded that as a model gap. The registry gains 29 ids, scene.wing.<wing> and scene.wing.<wing>.<tab>, each with exactly one literal render point that theme-slot.test.tsx enforces in both directions. Slot meaning and limits do not change: data only, one to four items per slot, 24 per package, nothing drawn when empty, no code, and no access to scene data. A slot is not the protected layer and cannot press or read a wing's controls.

- **H2 Context** (`context`)
  A theme package may place static images and plain text in 11 named slots, all on app pages and the scene backdrop and composer. The scene's wings are where a scene is set up, and a theme could restyle them through parts but could not add content to any of them. This is a gap in the model, not a defect, and a hand-rolled substitute is the wrong fix.
- **H2 Decision** (`decision`)
  slot-registry.ts stays the one list and parseSlots the one parser, gaining 29 ids: scene.wing.<wing> for each of nine wings and scene.wing.<wing>.<tab> for each tab. Each id has one literal ThemeSlot render point, and theme-slot.test.tsx fails when an id has none or a wing or tab has no id. Limits and meaning are unchanged: data only, one to four items per slot, 24 per package, nothing drawn when empty, no code and no scene data. A slot id survives component renames.
- **H2 Consequences** (`consequences`)
  A theme can brand or annotate any wing without touching its controls. A slot is not the protected layer and holds no behavior, so it cannot press or read anything in a wing. The 24-item package cap is unchanged, so more ids do not let a package draw more. Adding a wing or tab means adding its ids and one render point, which the test enforces.

### ADR-014: One reusable roleplay core for desktop and terminal hosts

- id: `decisions/ADR-014-reusable-roleplay-core`
- path: `docs/decisions/ADR-014-reusable-roleplay-core.md`
- The accepted consolidation direction retains one reusable turn engine and canonical scene model for desktop and terminal hosts. The source API exposes existing orchestration with injected stores, providers and library readers; skills, trackers, presets, macros, assembly and Final Cut keep their owners. The runtime owns only in-flight tasks, cancellation, gates and tracker rounds, while ChatStore owns durable scenes, checkpoints, versions and deltas. Hosts reuse a runtime per opened store, await accepted work before storage closes and refuse new work after shutdown. Host background promises must cover their writes. Imported scripts remain sealed under ADR-009, so terminal hosts without an isolated worker fail closed for applicable regex. Desktop consumes the API; the injected terminal runner is tested, while a standalone shared-library CLI remains withheld until cross-process checkpoint ownership is durable. Browser bundling and a scripted Node turn establish source compatibility only. Broader patch reconciliation and packaged verification remain outstanding, and old repository ancestry stays excluded.

- **H2 Decision** (`decision`)
  Bunzai absorbs co-developer improvements into its existing canonical owners rather than adopting a second default engine or journal. The public core injects storage, providers and readers. Its runtime owns cancellation, review and regex gates, tracker rounds and accepted tasks; ChatStore owns all persisted scene state. Hosts reuse one runtime for an opened store, await work before closing storage and admit no new operation after shutdown. afterEvent promises cover host writes. Other jobs remain host-owned, and imported regex requires ADR-009's isolated worker.
- **H2 Current implementation and limits** (`current-implementation-and-limits`)
  Desktop streaming consumes the reusable source API and explicit store-scoped runtime. The terminal runner supports injected send, continue and swipe operations with fail-closed regex tests. A standalone CLI is withheld because SQLite serialization cannot establish whether another process still owns a checkpoint; durable cross-process ownership remains required. Browser bundling and a scripted Node execution prove source behavior, not a packaged artifact or published library. Remaining co-developer patches are reconciled separately without importing the old repository's ancestry.

### ADR-015: Extension runtime and macro preview boundary

- id: `decisions/ADR-015-extension-runtime`
- path: `docs/decisions/ADR-015-extension-runtime.md`
- ADR-015 amends ADR-012 to permit one brokered macros.preview call from a user-invoked, manifest-declared command. Each request requires fresh host confirmation; the bounded sample and explicit value context are shown without evaluation, then the existing closed-world evaluator runs once with read-only state and regex disabled. The handler has no persistence, provider, nested broker or host-service access. Broker-path, static Pages Browser and real QuickJS/TUI Terminal evidence cover the narrow caller on those hosts; packaged WebView behavior and broader runtime or release readiness remain unverified.

- **H2 Amendment to ADR-012 Decision 4** (`amendment-to-adr-012-decision-4`)
  The amendment admits a fifth caller for the existing macro interpreter: an explicitly invoked manifest command calling macros.preview through the broker. The host reviews the bounded sample and supplied values before execution, and the handler evaluates only after approval, once, with read-only mode and regex disabled. It returns bounded rendered text and sanitized diagnostics without persisting state or invoking another capability.
- **H2 Acceptance evidence and limits** (`acceptance-evidence-and-limits`)
  Independent review accepted the narrow boundary. A broker-path test proves event activation is rejected, denial invokes the evaluator zero times, and approval invokes it once. The Desktop broker path, static Pages Browser probe and Terminal QuickJS/TUI path have been exercised. Those checks do not establish packaged WebView behavior, general host parity, package release readiness or completion of the extension runtime plan.

### ADR-016: Extension command registrations and prompt candidates

- id: `decisions/ADR-016-extension-command-and-prompt-hooks`
- path: `docs/decisions/ADR-016-extension-command-and-prompt-hooks.md`
- ADR-016 defines separate host-visible command registration and prompt-candidate operations. Commands remain manifest-declared; `commands.register` only replaces the installed package's enabled subset using trusted package identity and revision checks. Prompt hooks may contribute and then transform only their own invocation-local candidate, under separate grants and shared size/count bounds. Required failures abort before provider send; optional failures are counted by host control state and suspend after three consecutive failures. The ADR records the security semantics and evidence gates; it does not claim every host composition or release boundary is verified.

- **H2 Command registration** (`command-registration`)
  The manifest remains the identity and handler authority. Guest initialization binds handlers only to declared IDs; `commands.register` replaces the host-visible enabled subset with an exact manifest subset, using the current package identity and trusted commit revision. Install seeds all declared IDs to preserve existing behavior. The operation cannot add handlers, rename commands, change grants or control another package, and all activation paths must honor the stored subset.
- **H2 Prompt contribution and transformation** (`prompt-contribution-and-transformation`)
  Prompt hooks run only at a host-owned active hook. A package may contribute one bounded candidate and later transform only that same package/hook candidate; it cannot replace the assembled prompt. Operations use separate grants and invocation-local state. Prompt activation admits only operations explicitly marked safe for that origin and still applies ordinary exact grants. Candidates are ordered deterministically and share count/byte limits. Required hook failure aborts before provider send; optional failures use host-owned control state, reset on success and suspend after three consecutive failures. Revocation is checked before accepting output, and failed or stale candidates are discarded.
- **H2 Evidence required** (`evidence-required`)
  The ADR names the required evidence: exact command-subset validation and isolation, install defaults, disabled-command rejection on each composed host, per-hook contribution and transformation isolation, independent grants and common bounds. Catalog and UI metadata must come from source definitions. These are acceptance requirements, not proof that all host integrations or packaged behavior have passed.

## guide

### Convert a card

- id: `guide/converting`
- path: `docs/guide/converting.md`
- User guide for converting a card from one app's format to another. Most content crosses cleanly through one shared middle model, but private scripts and fields without a target home stay behind on purpose. Short workflow: import into Library with receipt, optionally edit on Workbench, stage for the Press, pick one target format, run the press and download a platform-named zip. Same-app round-trip keeps everything via the saved original file including private extensions layout and ids. Cross-app keeps only shared model fields; one app's private scripts never copy into another; scripts drop when converting to plainer apps like Agnai or legacy Backyard while name description and greetings arrive; use Risu .charx when the full rule pack must travel. Export honesty view lists keep note and drop lines before commit. Press batch stages many pieces one target per run with per-row printed skipped or failed results; staged characters bring linked lorebooks as riders. Tips stress same-app prefer, read drop notes, and fix lorebook keywords before export because empty keywords never fire and Press flags that in red.

- **H2 The short version** (`the-short-version`)
  Five steps: import the card into the Library by drop or Import files reading the receipt and checking desired files; open on Workbench to edit or skip; stage for the Press via right-click or Press left rail, with staged characters bringing linked lorebooks; pick one target platform in the Press; click run the press then download the bundle as a single zip named for the platform.
- **H2 What survives and what drops** (`what-survives-and-what-drops`)
  Every convert passes through one shared model: read in then write out in the target shape.
